has the block format

X=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}X_{00}&X_{01}&X_{02}&X_{03}\\
\hline\cr X_{10}&X_{11}&X_{12}&X_{13}\\
\hline\cr X_{20}&X_{21}&X_{22}&X_{23}\\
\hline\cr X_{30}&X_{31}&X_{32}&X_{33}\\
\end{array}\right)\!,

(2.59)

where the degree
x_{ij}
of the block
X_{ij}
is

\displaystyle x_{ij}=\mathchar 28941\relax_{i}+\mathchar 28941\relax_{j}+\mathchar 28941\relax_{k}.

(2.60)

Since the entries of the
X_{ij}
are real numbers and so of degree
\mathchar 28941\relax_{0}
, all the blocks with non-vanishing
x_{ij}
do vanish. For instance, if
\mathchar 28941\relax_{k}=01\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
(resp.,
\mathchar 28941\relax_{k}=11
) (do not confuse with the row-column index
01
in
X_{01}
(resp.,
11
in
X_{11}
)), the degree
x_{ij}=0
if and only if
ij\in\{01,10,23,32\}
(resp.,
ij\in\{03,12,21,30\}
) (as in most of the other cases in this text, the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-degrees are lexicographically ordered), so that only these
X_{ij}
do not vanish. It follows that

\underline{\Hom}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}\tt Vec}\big(\mathbf{R}^{p|\underline{q}},\mathbf{R}^{r|\underline{s}}\big)=\operatorname{gl}\big(r|\underline{s}\times p|\underline{q},{\mathbb{R}}\big)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt FinVec}

is made of the matrices (
2.59
), where no block
X_{ij}
vanishes a priori. The canonical basis of this
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-vector space are the obvious matrices
E_{ik,j\ell}
(
i,j\in\{0,\dots,N\}
,
k\in\{1,\dots,s_{i}\}
,
\ell\in\{1,\dots,q_{j}\}
) with all entries equal to 0 except the entry
kl
in
X_{ij}
which is
1
. In view of equation (
2.60
), the vectors of this basis have the degrees
\mathchar 28941\relax_{i}+\mathchar 28941\relax_{j}
. We can of course identify (up to renumbering) this
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-vector space with
\mathbf{R}^{t|\underline{u}}
, where
u_{n}
(
n\in\{0,\dots,N\}
) is equal to

\displaystyle u_{n}=\sum_{i,j\colon\mathchar 28941\relax_{i}+\mathchar 28941\relax_{j}=\mathchar 28941\relax_{n}}s_{i}q_{j}

(2.61)

(we set
s_{0}=r
,
q_{0}=p
,
u_{0}:=t
). Hence:

\displaystyle\underline{\Hom}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}\tt Vec}\big(\mathbf{R}^{p|\underline{q}},\mathbf{R}^{r|\underline{s}}\big)=\operatorname{gl}\big(r|\underline{s}\times p|\underline{q},{\mathbb{R}}\big)=\mathbf{R}^{t|\underline{u}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt CarVec}.

(2.62)

Combining (
2.58
) and (
2.62
), we get

\displaystyle\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}\tt LinMan}\big({\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}},{\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}\big)=\mathcal{M}\big(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}\tt Vec}\big(\mathbf{R}^{p|\underline{q}},\mathbf{R}^{r|\underline{s}}\big)\big)={\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt CarMan}.

(2.63)

We now come back to (
\diamond
). Setting as usual
{\mathbb{R}}^{0|\underline{m}}\simeq\mathchar 28675\relax
, we get the isomorphism

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}\tt LinMan}\big({\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}},{\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)={\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)\simeq\Pi_{n=0}^{N}\,\mathchar 28675\relax_{\mathchar 28941\relax_{n}}^{\times u_{n}}

of Fréchet
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-modules. On the other hand, the vector space
\operatorname{gl}_{0}\big(r|\underline{s}\times p|\underline{q},\mathchar 28675\relax\big)
is a
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-module and this module “coincides” obviously with

\operatorname{gl}_{0}\big(r|\underline{s}\times p|\underline{q},\mathchar 28675\relax\big)=\Pi_{n=0}^{N}\,\mathchar 28675\relax_{\mathchar 28941\relax_{n}}^{\times u_{n}}.

By transferring the Fréchet structure, we get an “equality” of Fréchet
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-modules. Hence, the Fréchet
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-module isomorphism

\displaystyle\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}\tt LinMan}\big({\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}},{\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)={\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)\simeq\Pi_{n=0}^{N}\,\mathchar 28675\relax_{\mathchar 28941\relax_{n}}^{\times u_{n}}=\operatorname{gl}_{0}\big(r|\underline{s}\times p|\underline{q},\mathchar 28675\relax\big)\in{\tt F}\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}{\tt Mod}.

(2.64)

There is a natural upgrade that is independent of the internal Homs and makes
G:=\operatorname{gl}_{0}(r|\underline{s}\times p|\underline{q},-)
a functor
G\in{\tt Fun_{0}}\big({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt FAMod}\big)
. Indeed, it suffices to define
G
on a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Alg}
-morphism
\mathchar 28967\relax^{*}\colon\mathchar 28675\relax\to\mathchar 28675\relax^{\prime}
as

G(\mathchar 28967\relax^{*})\colon\ G(\mathchar 28675\relax)\ni X\mapsto\mathchar 28967\relax^{*}(X)\in G(\mathchar 28675\relax^{\prime}),

where
\mathchar 28967\relax^{*}(X)
is defined entry-wise. The morphism
G(\mathchar 28967\relax^{*})
is clearly
(\mathchar 28967\relax^{*})_{0}
-linear. It is also continuous, as it can be viewed as a product of copies of
\mathchar 28967\relax^{*}
. Since
G
respects compositions and identities it is actually a functor of the functor category mentioned. The functors
G
and
{\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}(-)=\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}})
are of course naturally isomorphic. Since
\mathcal{S}
is a fully faithful functor

\mathcal{S}\colon\ {\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt LinMan}\to{\tt Fun_{0}}\big({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt FAMod}\big),

the functor
G
can be viewed as represented by the linear
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifold
{\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}
.

Proposition 2.29
.

The functor
\operatorname{gl}_{0}(r|\underline{s}\times p|\underline{q},-)
is representable and the Cartesian
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifold

\operatorname{gl}_{0}\big(r|\underline{s}\times p|\underline{q}\big):={\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}},

with dimension
t|\underline{u}
defined in equation (
2.61
), is “its” representing object.

Example 2.30
.

For
n=2
, we find that
\operatorname{gl}_{0}(1|1,1,1)={\mathbb{R}}^{4|4,4,4}
.

3
\boldsymbol{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}}
-Lie groups and linear actions

3.1
\boldsymbol{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}}
-matrices

We will consider matrices that are valued in some
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-Grassmann algebra
\Lambda
, though everything we say generalizes to arbitrary
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-commutative associative unital
{\mathbb{R}}
-algebras. A homogeneous matrix
X\in\operatorname{gl}_{x}\big(r|\underline{s}\times p|\underline{q},\Lambda\big)
of degree
x\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
is understood to be a block matrix

X=\begin{pmatrix}X_{00}&\dotsc&X_{0N}\\
\hline\cr\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\hline\cr X_{N0}&\dotsc&X_{NN}\end{pmatrix}\!,

with the entries of each block
X_{ij}
being elements of the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-Grassmann algebra
\Lambda
. Here the degree
x_{ij}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
of
X_{ij}
is

x_{ij}=\gamma_{i}+\gamma_{j}+x

and the dimension of
X_{ij}
is

\dim(X_{ij})=s_{i}\times q_{j}

(setting
s_{0}=r
and
q_{0}=p
as usual). Addition of such matrices and multiplication by reals are defined in the obvious way and they endow
\operatorname{gl}_{x}\big(r|\underline{s}\times p|\underline{q},\Lambda\big)
with a vector space structure. We set

\operatorname{gl}\big(r|\underline{s}\times p|\underline{q},\Lambda\big):=\bigoplus_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}}\operatorname{gl}_{x}\big(r|\underline{s}\times p|\underline{q},\Lambda\big)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Vec}.

Multiplication by an element of
\Lambda
requires an extra sign factor given by the row of the matrix, i.e., for any homogeneous
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathchar 28941\relax_{k}}
, we have that

(\lambda\>X)_{ij}=(-1)^{\langle\mathchar 28941\relax_{k},\gamma_{i}\rangle}\lambda\>X_{ij}.

We thus obtain on
\operatorname{gl}\big(r|\underline{s}\times p|\underline{q},\Lambda\big)
a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-graded module structure over the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-commutative algebra
\mathchar 28675\relax
. If
r|\underline{s}=p|\underline{q}
, we write

\operatorname{gl}\big(p|\underline{q},\mathchar 28675\relax\big):=\operatorname{gl}(p|\underline{q}\times p|\underline{q},\mathchar 28675\relax).

Multiplication of matrices in
\operatorname{gl}(p|\underline{q},\Lambda)
is via standard matrix multiplication – now taking care that the entries are from a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-commutative algebra. Equipped with this multiplication, the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-graded
\mathchar 28675\relax
-module
\operatorname{gl}\big(p|\underline{q},\mathchar 28675\relax\big)
is a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-graded associative unital
{\mathbb{R}}
-algebra. In particular, the degree zero matrices
\operatorname{gl}_{0}\big(p|\underline{q},\mathchar 28675\relax\big)
form an associative unital
{\mathbb{R}}
-algebra. Since multiplication of matrices only uses multiplication and addition in
\mathchar 28675\relax
, we can replace
\mathchar 28675\relax
not only, as said above, by any
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-commutative associative unital
{\mathbb{R}}
-algebra, but also by any
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-commutative ring
R
and then get a ring
\operatorname{gl}_{0}(p|\underline{q},R)
. We denote by
\GL(p|\underline{q},R)
the group of invertible matrices in
\operatorname{gl}_{0}(p|\underline{q},R)
. For further details the reader may consult
[
23
]
.

3.2
Invertibility of
\boldsymbol{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}}
-matrices

Let
R
be a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-commutative ring which is Hausdorff-complete in the
J
-adic topology, where

J
is the (proper) homogeneous ideal of
R
that is generated by the elements of non-zero degree
\gamma_{j}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,N\}
. The
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-graded ring morphism

\varepsilon\colon R\to\,R/J
, where

R/J=\bigoplus_{i}R_{i}/(R_{i}\cap J)=R_{0}/(R_{0}\cap J)

vanishes in all non-zero degrees, induces a ring morphism

\tilde{\varepsilon}\colon\ \operatorname{gl}_{0}\big(p|\underline{q},R\big)\ni X\mapsto\tilde{\varepsilon}(X)\in\operatorname{Diag}\big(p|\underline{q},R/J\big),

where
\tilde{\varepsilon}(X)
is the
block-diagonal matrix with diagonal blocks
\tilde{\varepsilon}(X_{ii})
(with commuting entries).

The following proposition appeared as Proposition 5.1 in
[
22
]
:

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
R
be a
J
-adically Hausdorff-complete
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-commutative ring and let
X\in\operatorname{gl}_{0}\big(p|\underline{q},R\big)
be a degree zero
p|{\underline{q}}\times p|{\underline{q}}
matrix with entries in
R
, written in the
standard block format

X=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}X_{00}&\dotsc&X_{0N}\\
\hline\cr\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\hline\cr X_{N0}&\dotsc&X_{NN}\\
\end{array}\right)\!.

We have:

\displaystyle X\in\GL(p|\underline{q},R)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow X_{ii}\in\GL(q_{i},R),\quad\forall i

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\tilde{\varepsilon}(X)\in\GL(p|\underline{q},R/J)\Leftrightarrow\tilde{\varepsilon}(X_{ii})\in\GL(q_{i},R/J),\quad\forall i.

In this work, we are of course mainly interested in the case
R:=\mathchar 28675\relax={\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathring{\mathchar 28675\relax}
and
J=\mathring{\mathchar 28675\relax}
, so that
R/J={\mathbb{R}}
.

3.3
\boldsymbol{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}}
-Lie groups and their functor of points

Groups, or, better, group objects can easily be defined in any category with finite products, i.e., any category
\tt C
with terminal object
1
and binary categorical products
c\times c^{\prime}
(
c,c^{\prime}\in{\tt C}
).

If
\tt C
is a concrete category, the definition of a group object is very simple. For instance, if
\tt C
is the concrete category
A\tt FM
of Fréchet manifolds over a Fréchet algebra
A
, a group object
\mathcal{G}
in
\tt C
is just an object
\mathcal{G}\in\tt C
that is group whose structure maps
\mathchar 28950\relax\colon\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
and
\operatorname{inv}\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
are
\tt C
-morphisms, i.e.,
A
-smooth maps. We refer of course to a group object in
A\tt FM
as a
Fréchet
A
-Lie group
.

If
\tt C
is the category
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}\tt Man
of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifolds, the definition of a group object is similar, but all the (natural) requirements (above) have to be expressed in terms of arrows (since there are no points here). More precisely, a group object
G
in
\tt C
is an object
G\in\tt C
that comes equipped with
\tt C
-morphisms

\mathchar 28950\relax\colon\ G\times G\to G,\qquad\operatorname{inv}\colon\ G\to G\qquad\text{and}\qquad e\colon\ 1\to G

(the terminal object
1
is here the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifold
{\mathbb{R}}^{0|\underline{0}}=(\{\star\},{\mathbb{R}})
), which are called multiplication, inverse and unit, and satisfy the standard group properties (expressed by means of arrows):
\mathchar 28950\relax
is associative,
\operatorname{inv}
is a two-sided inverse of
\mathchar 28950\relax
and
e
is a two-sided unit of
\mathchar 28950\relax
. To understand the arrow expressions of these properties, we need the following notations. We denote by
\mathchar 28673\relax\colon G\to G\times G
the canonical diagonal
\tt C
-morphism and we denote by
e_{G}\colon G\to G
the composite of the unique
\tt C
-morphism
1_{G}\colon G\to 1
and the unit
\tt C
-morphism
e\colon 1\to G
. The left inverse condition now reads

\mathchar 28950\relax\circ(\operatorname{inv}\times\operatorname{id}_{G})\circ\mathchar 28673\relax=e_{G}

and the left unit condition reads

\mathchar 28950\relax\circ(e_{G}\times\operatorname{id}_{G})\circ\mathchar 28673\relax=\operatorname{id}_{G}

(and similarly for the right conditions). The associativity of
\mathchar 28950\relax
is of course encoded by

\displaystyle\mathchar 28950\relax\circ(\mathchar 28950\relax\times\operatorname{id}_{G})=\mathchar 28950\relax\circ(\operatorname{id}_{G}\times\mathchar 28950\relax).

(3.1)

We refer to a group object in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}\tt Man
as a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-Lie group
.

A morphism
F\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
of Fréchet
A
-Lie groups is of course defined as an
A
-smooth map that is a group morphism. Analogously, a morphism
F\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
from a Fréchet
A
-Lie group to a Fréchet
A^{\prime}
-Lie group is a morphism of
\tt AFM
that is also a group morphism. We denote the
category of Fréchet
A
-Lie groups
by
A\tt FLg
and we write
\tt AFLg
for the
category of Fréchet Lie groups over any Fréchet algebra
.

Further, a morphism
\Phi\colon G\to G^{\prime}
of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-Lie groups is a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphism that respects the multiplications, the inverses and the units (obvious arrow definitions). The
category of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-Lie groups
we denote by
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}\tt Lg
.

The functor of points of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifolds

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\colon\ {\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man}\to{\tt Fun}_{0}\big({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt AFM}\big)

(3.2)

induces a fully faithful functor of points of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-Lie groups:

Theorem 3.2
.

The functor

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\colon\ {\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Lg}\to{\tt Fun}_{0}\big({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt AFLg}\big)

(3.3)

is fully faithful. Moreover, if
M\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man}
and

\mathcal{S}(M)=M(-)\in{\tt Fun}_{0}\big({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt AFLg}\big),

then
M\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Lg}
.

This theorem was announced as
[
13
, Theorem 3.30]
without proper explanation or proof.

Proof.

It is clear that we have subcategories

{\tt AFLg}\subset{\tt AFM},\qquad{\tt Fun}_{0}\big({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt AFLg}\big)\subset{\tt Fun}_{0}\big({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt AFM}\big)\qquad\text{and}\qquad{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}\tt Lg}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man}.

Therefore, in order to prove that the functor (
3.2
) restricts to a functor (
3.3
), it suffices to show that
\mathcal{S}
sends objects
G
and morphisms
\Phi
of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Lg}
to objects and morphisms of the functor category with target
{\tt AFLg}
.
Observe first that, for any
M,N\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man}
, we have the functor equality

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(M\times N)=(M\times N)(-)=M(-)\times N(-)=\mathcal{S}(M)\times\mathcal{S}(N),

(3.4)

in view of the universal property of
M\times N
. Further, if
\phi\colon M\to M^{\prime}
and
\psi\colon N\to N^{\prime}
are two
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphisms, the natural transformation

\mathcal{S}(\phi\times\psi)=(\phi\times\psi)_{-}\colon\ (M\times N)(-)\to(M^{\prime}\times N^{\prime})(-)

becomes
\phi_{-}\times\psi_{-}
, if we read it through the identification (
3.4
).

Now, if
G\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Lg}
with structure
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphisms
\mathchar 28950\relax
,
\operatorname{inv}
(and
e
), then the
{\tt AFM}
-valued functor
\mathcal{S}(G)=G(-)
is actually
{\tt AFLg}
-valued. This means that it sends any
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-Grassmann algebra
\mathchar 28675\relax
and any
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Alg}
-morphism
\mathchar 28967\relax^{*}\colon\mathchar 28675\relax\to\mathchar 28675\relax^{\prime}
to an object
G(\mathchar 28675\relax)
and a morphism
G(\mathchar 28967\relax^{*})
of
\tt AFLg
.

For
G(\mathchar 28675\relax)\in\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}{\tt FM}
, notice that the natural transformations
\mathcal{S}(\mathchar 28950\relax)=\mathchar 28950\relax_{-},\;\mathcal{S}(\operatorname{inv})=\operatorname{inv}_{-}
(and
\mathcal{S}(e)=e_{-}
) have
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-smooth
\mathchar 28675\relax
-components

\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\colon\ G(\mathchar 28675\relax)\times G(\mathchar 28675\relax)\to G(\mathchar 28675\relax),\qquad\operatorname{inv}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\colon\ G(\mathchar 28675\relax)\to G(\mathchar 28675\relax)\qquad(\text{and }e_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\colon\ 1(\mathchar 28675\relax)\to G(\mathchar 28675\relax))

(the Fréchet
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-manifold
1(\mathchar 28675\relax)
is the singleton that consists of the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Alg}
-morphism
\iota_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
that sends any real number to itself viewed as an element of
\mathchar 28675\relax
) that define a group structure on
G(\mathchar 28675\relax)
(with unit
1_{\mathchar 28675\relax}:=e_{\mathchar 28675\relax}(\iota_{\mathchar 28675\relax})
), which is therefore a Fréchet
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-Lie group. The group properties of these structure maps are consequences of the group properties of the structure maps of
G
. For instance, when we apply
\mathcal{S}
to the associativity equation (
3.1
) and then take the
\mathchar 28675\relax
-component of the resulting natural transformation, we get

\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\circ\big(\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\times\operatorname{id}_{G(\mathchar 28675\relax)}\!\big)=\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\circ\big(\operatorname{id}_{G(\mathchar 28675\relax)}\times\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\big).

As for
G(\mathchar 28967\relax^{*})\colon G(\mathchar 28675\relax)\to G(\mathchar 28675\relax^{\prime})
, we know that it is an
{\tt AFM}
-morphism and have to show that it respects the multiplications
\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
and
\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax^{\prime}}
, i.e., that

\displaystyle\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax^{\prime}}\circ(G(\mathchar 28967\relax^{*})\times G(\mathchar 28967\relax^{*}))=G(\mathchar 28967\relax^{*})\circ\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}.

(3.5)

However, this equality is nothing other than the naturalness property of
\mathchar 28950\relax_{-}
.

Finally, let
\Phi\colon G\to G^{\prime}
be a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Lg}
-morphism and denote the multiplications of the source and target by
\mathchar 28950\relax
and
\mathchar 28950\relax^{\prime}
, respectively. In order to prove that the natural transformation
\mathcal{S}(\Phi)=\Phi_{-}\colon G(-)\to G^{\prime}(-)
of the functor category with target
{\tt AFM}
is a natural transformation of the functor category with target
{\tt AFLg}
, it suffices to show that
\Phi_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
is a morphism of
{\tt AFLg}
, which results from the application of the functor
\mathcal{S}
to the commutative diagram

\displaystyle\mathchar 28950\relax^{\prime}\circ(\Phi\times\Phi)=\Phi\circ\mathchar 28950\relax.

(3.6)

The next task is to show that the functor (
3.3
) is fully faithful, i.e., that the map

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{G,G^{\prime}}\colon\ \operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Lg}}(G,G^{\prime})\ni\Phi\mapsto\Phi_{-}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\tt Fun}_{0}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}},\,{\tt AFLg})}(G(-),G^{\prime}(-))

(3.7)

is a
1:1
correspondence, for any
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-Lie groups
G,G^{\prime}
. Since the functor (
3.2
) is fully faithful, any natural transformation in the target set of (
3.7
) is implemented by a unique
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphism
\phi\colon G\to G^{\prime}
and it suffices to show that
\phi
respects the group operations, for instance, that is satisfies equation (
3.6
). However, equation (
3.6
) is satisfied if and only if

\mathchar 28950\relax^{\prime}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\circ(\phi_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\times\phi_{\mathchar 28675\relax})=\phi_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\circ\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax},

for all
\mathchar 28675\relax
. The latter condition holds, since
\phi_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
is, by assumption, a group morphism.

We must still prove the last statement of Theorem
3.2
. The assumption implies that, for any
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-Grassmann algebra
\mathchar 28675\relax
and any
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-algebra morphism
\mathchar 28967\relax^{*}\colon\mathchar 28675\relax\to\mathchar 28675\relax^{\prime}
, we get a Fréchet
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-Lie group
M(\mathchar 28675\relax)
and a
(\mathchar 28967\relax^{*})_{0}
-smooth group morphism
M(\mathchar 28967\relax^{*})\colon M(\mathchar 28675\relax)\to M(\mathchar 28675\relax^{\prime})
. We denote by
1_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
(resp.,
\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax},\operatorname{inv}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
) the unit element (resp., the
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-smooth multiplication, the
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-smooth inverse) of the group structure on the Fréchet
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-manifold
M(\mathchar 28675\relax)
. We have already observed (see (
3.5
)) that the fact that
M(\mathchar 28967\relax^{*})
respects the multiplications
\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
and
\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax^{\prime}}
is equivalent to that of
\mathchar 28950\relax_{-}
being natural. The natural transformation
\mathchar 28950\relax_{-}\colon(M\times M)(-)\to M(-)
is implemented by a unique
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphism
\mathchar 28950\relax\colon M\times M\to M
. We obtain similarly a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphism
\operatorname{inv}\colon M\to M
. As for
e\colon 1\to M
, we notice that the maps

e_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\colon\ 1(\mathchar 28675\relax)\ni\iota_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\mapsto 1_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\in M(\mathchar 28675\relax)\quad(\mathchar 28675\relax\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt GrAlg})

define visibly a natural transformation with
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-smooth
\mathchar 28675\relax
-components. Hence, it is implemented by a unique
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphism
e\colon 1\to M
. We leave it to the reader to check that
\mathchar 28950\relax
,
\operatorname{inv}
and
e
satisfy (
3.1
) and the other group properties. ∎

3.4
The general linear
\boldsymbol{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}}
-group

We want to define the
general linear
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-group
of order
p|\underline{q}
so that it is a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-Lie group
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)
. In view of Theorem
3.2
, it suffices to define a functor

\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(-)\in{\tt Fun}_{0}\big({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt AFLg}\big)\;

that is represented by a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifold
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)
.

Definition 3.3
.

The
general linear
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-group

\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)
is defined, for any
\mathchar 28675\relax\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt GrAlg}
, by

\GL\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\Lambda):=\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q},\mathchar 28675\relax\big)=\big\{X\in\operatorname{gl}_{0}(p|\underline{q},\mathchar 28675\relax)\colon X\textnormal{ is invertible}\big\},

and, for any
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Alg}
-morphism
\mathchar 28967\relax^{*}\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda^{\prime}
and any
X\in\GL\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\Lambda)
, by

\displaystyle\GL\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\varphi^{*})(X):=\tilde{\varphi}^{*}X,

where
\tilde{\mathchar 28967\relax}^{*}
is
\mathchar 28967\relax^{*}
acting on
X
entry-by-entry.

Theorem 3.4
.

The maps
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(-)
of Definition
3.3
define a representable functor. We refer to the representing object
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Lg}
as the
general linear
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-group of dimension

p|\underline{q}
.

Proof.

Recall that:

1.

It follows from equation (
2.64
) that

\operatorname{gl}_{0}\big(p|\underline{q},\mathchar 28675\relax\big)=\Pi_{n=0}^{N}\mathchar 28675\relax_{\mathchar 28941\relax_{n}}^{\times u_{n}}=\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}^{\times t}\times\Pi_{j=1}^{N}\mathchar 28675\relax_{\mathchar 28941\relax_{j}}^{\times u_{j}}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28675\relax),

where
u_{n}
is given by (
2.61
) (
t=u_{0}
).

2.

It follows from Proposition
3.1
that
X\in\operatorname{gl}_{0}\big(p|\underline{q},\mathchar 28675\relax\big)
is invertible if and only if
\tilde{\mathchar 28962\relax}(X)\in\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q},{\mathbb{R}}\big)
, if and only if
\tilde{\mathchar 28962\relax}(X_{ii})\in\operatorname{GL}(q_{i},{\mathbb{R}})
, for all
i\in\{0,\dots,N\}
, if and only if
X_{ii}\in\operatorname{GL}(q_{i},\mathchar 28675\relax)
, for all
i\in\{0,\dots,N\}
.

In particular, a matrix

X\in\operatorname{gl}_{0}\big(p|\underline{q},{\mathbb{R}}\big)={\mathbb{R}}^{t}={\mathbb{R}}^{p^{2}+\sum_{j}q_{j}^{2}}=\operatorname{Diag}\big(p|\underline{q},{\mathbb{R}}\big)

is invertible if and only if
X_{ii}\in\operatorname{GL}(q_{i},{\mathbb{R}})
, for all
i
. It follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{t}:=\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)({\mathbb{R}})=\Pi_{i=0}^{N}\operatorname{GL}(q_{i},{\mathbb{R}})\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{t}.

(3.8)

As
\mathcal{U}^{t}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{t}
is open, we can consider the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-domain

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}:=(\mathcal{U}^{t},\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}}|_{\mathcal{U}^{t}}),

(3.9)

as well as its functor of points

\mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}(-)\in{\tt Fun}_{0}\big({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt AFM}\big),

with value on
\mathchar 28675\relax

\mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)\simeq\mathcal{U}^{t}\times\mathring{\mathchar 28675\relax}^{\times t}_{0}\times\Pi_{j=1}^{N}\mathchar 28675\relax_{\mathchar 28941\relax_{j}}^{\times u_{j}}\;

(see
[
13
]
).

On the other hand, we get

\displaystyle\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)

\displaystyle=\big\{X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{t}\times\mathring{\mathchar 28675\relax}^{\times t}_{0}\times\Pi_{j=1}^{N}\mathchar 28675\relax_{\mathchar 28941\relax_{j}}^{\times u_{j}}\colon(\dots,\tilde{\mathchar 28962\relax}(X_{ii}),\dots)\in\Pi_{i=0}^{N}\operatorname{GL}(q_{i},{\mathbb{R}})\big\}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}^{t}\times\mathring{\mathchar 28675\relax}^{\times t}_{0}\times\Pi_{j=1}^{N}\mathchar 28675\relax_{\mathchar 28941\relax_{j}}^{\times u_{j}},

so that
\mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}(-)
and
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(-)
“coincide” on objects
\mathchar 28675\relax
: if we denote the coordinates of
{\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}
as usually by
(u^{\mathfrak{a}})=\big(x^{a},\mathchar 28952\relax^{A}\big)
, this “equality” reads

\mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)\ni{\rm x}^{*}\simeq({\rm x}^{*}(u^{\mathfrak{a}}))_{\mathfrak{a}}\in\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax).

Moreover,
\mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}(-)
and
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(-)
coincide on morphisms
\mathchar 28967\relax^{*}\colon\mathchar 28675\relax\to\mathchar 28675\relax^{\prime}
. Indeed, the map
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28967\relax^{*})
acts on a matrix

({\rm x}^{*}(u^{\mathfrak{a}}))_{\mathfrak{a}}\in\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)\subset\mathchar 28675\relax^{\times t}_{0}\times\Pi_{j=1}^{N}\mathchar 28675\relax_{\mathchar 28941\relax_{j}}^{\times u_{j}}\;

by acting on all its entries
{\rm x}^{*}(u^{\mathfrak{a}})
by
\mathchar 28967\relax^{*}
, whereas the map
\mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28967\relax^{*})
acts on a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Alg}
-morphism
{\rm x}^{*}\in\mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)
by left composition
\mathchar 28967\relax^{*}\circ{\rm x}^{*}
; if we identify
{\rm x}^{*}
with the tuple
({\rm x}^{*}(u^{\mathfrak{a}}))_{\mathfrak{a}}
, then
\mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28967\relax^{*})
acts by acting on each
{\rm x}^{*}(u^{\mathfrak{a}})
by
\mathchar 28967\relax^{*}
, which proves the claim.

It follows that
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(-)
is a functor

\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(-)\in{\tt Fun}_{0}\big({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt AFM}\big)

that is represented by

\displaystyle\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big):=\mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man},

(3.10)

so that it now suffices to prove that this functor is valued in
{\tt AFLg}
, i.e., it suffices to show that
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)\in\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}{\tt FLg}
and that
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28967\relax^{*})
is an
{\tt AFLg}
-morphism.

Recall that
\operatorname{gl}_{0}\big(p|\underline{q},\mathchar 28675\relax\big)
is an associative unital
{\mathbb{R}}
-algebra for the standard matrix multiplication
\cdot
(standard matrix addition, standard matrix multiplication by reals and standard unit matrix
\mathbb{I}
) (see Section
3.1
). It is clear that the subset
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)\subset\operatorname{gl}_{0}\big(p|\underline{q},\mathchar 28675\relax\big)
is closed under
\cdot
:

\displaystyle\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\colon\ \operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)\times\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)\ni(X,Y)\mapsto X\cdot Y\in\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)

(3.11)

is an associative unital multiplication on
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)
. Therefore,
\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
and

\displaystyle\operatorname{inv}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\colon\ \operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)\ni X\mapsto X^{-1}\in\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)

(3.12)

endow
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)
with a group structure (with unit
\mathbb{I}
). Finally, the Fréchet
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-manifold
\mathop{\rm GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)
together with its group structure
\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax},\operatorname{inv}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
(and
\mathbb{I}
) is a Fréchet
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-Lie group, if its structure maps
\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
and
\operatorname{inv}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
are
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-smooth. This condition is actually satisfied (see below).

As for
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28967\relax^{*})
, we know that it is an
{\tt AFM}
-morphism and need to show that it respects the multiplications
\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
,
\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax^{\prime}}
. This condition is clearly met because
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28967\relax^{*})
acts entry-wise by the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Alg}
-morphism
\mathchar 28967\relax^{*}
.

It remains to explain why
\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
and
\operatorname{inv}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
are
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-smooth.

Notice first that the source of the multiplication (
3.11
) is the open subset
\mathchar 28682\relax(\mathchar 28675\relax):=\mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)\times\mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)
of the Fréchet space
F(\mathchar 28675\relax):={\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)
(see
[
13
]
) and that we can choose the Fréchet vector space (and Fréchet
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-module)
{\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)
as its target. Since
\mathchar 28675\relax
is the (
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-commutative nuclear) Fréchet
{\mathbb{R}}
-algebra of global
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-functions of some
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-point
{\mathbb{R}}^{0|\underline{m}}
, its addition and internal multiplication (its multiplication by reals and subtraction) are continuous maps. It follows that each component function of the standard matrix multiplication
\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
is continuous, so that
\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
is itself continuous. We must now explain why all directional derivatives of
\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
exist everywhere and are continuous, and why the first derivative is
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-linear. Let
(X,Y)\in\mathchar 28682\relax(\mathchar 28675\relax)
and
(V,W)\in F(\mathchar 28675\relax)
. We get

{\rm d}_{(X,Y)}\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\,(V,W)=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{(X+tV)\cdot(Y+tW)-X\cdot Y}{t}=X\cdot W+V\cdot Y.

Hence, the first derivative exists everywhere, is continuous and
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-linear. Indeed, for any
{\rm a}\in\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
, we have

{\rm d}_{(X,Y)}\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\,({\rm a}\cdot V,{\rm a}\cdot W)={\rm a}\cdot{\rm d}_{(X,Y)}\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\,(V,W).

It is easily checked that

{\rm d}^{2}_{(X,Y)}\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}(V_{1},W_{1},V_{2},W_{2})=V_{2}\cdot W_{1}+V_{1}\cdot W_{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{}^{k\geq 3}_{(X,Y)}\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}(V_{1},W_{1},\dots,V_{k},W_{k})=0,

so that
\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
is actually
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-smooth.

As for

\operatorname{inv}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\colon\ \mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28675\relax),

we start computing the directional derivative of

\mathbb{I}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}:=\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\circ(\operatorname{inv}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\times\operatorname{id}_{\mathchar 28675\relax})\circ\mathchar 28673\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\colon\ \mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)\ni X\mapsto X^{-1}\cdot X=\mathbb{I}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)

(
\mathchar 28673\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
is the diagonal map), assuming continuity of
\operatorname{inv}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
, for the time being. For any
V\in{\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)
, we have

{\rm d}_{X}\mathbb{I}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}(V)=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{(X+tV)^{-1}\cdot(X+tV)-X^{-1}\cdot X}{t}=\lim_{t\to 0}\left(f_{XV}(t)\cdot X+g_{XV}(t)\cdot V\right)=0,

where

f_{XV}(t)=\frac{(X+tV)^{-1}-X^{-1}}{t}\qquad\text{and}\qquad g_{XV}(t)=(X+tV)^{-1}.

It follows that

\displaystyle{\rm d}_{X}\operatorname{inv}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}(V)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0}f_{XV}(t)=\lim_{t\to 0}\big((f_{XV}(t)\cdot X+g_{XV}(t)\cdot V)\cdot X^{-1}-g_{XV}(t)\cdot V\cdot X^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=-X^{-1}\cdot V\cdot X^{-1},

so that the first derivative is defined everywhere, is continuous, as well as
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-linear. Also the higher order derivatives exist everywhere and are continuous. For instance, the second order derivative is given by

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{2}_{X}\operatorname{inv}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}(V,W)

\displaystyle=-\lim_{t\to 0}\big(f_{XW}(t)\cdot V\cdot X^{-1}+g_{XW}(t)\cdot V\cdot f_{XW}(t)\big)

\displaystyle=X^{-1}\cdot W\cdot X^{-1}\cdot V\cdot X^{-1}+X^{-1}\cdot V\cdot X^{-1}\cdot W\cdot X^{-1}.

Finally, the inverse map
\operatorname{inv}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
is
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-smooth, provided we prove its still pending continuity.

We will show that the continuity of (
3.12
) boils down to the continuity of the inverse map
\iota_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\colon\mathchar 28675\relax^{\times}\ni\mathchar 28949\relax\mapsto\mathchar 28949\relax^{-1}\in\mathchar 28675\relax^{\times}
in
\mathchar 28675\relax
. Here
\mathchar 28675\relax^{\times}\subset\mathchar 28675\relax
is the group of invertible elements of
\mathchar 28675\relax
. Since
\mathchar 28675\relax
is a (unital) Fréchet
{\mathbb{R}}
-algebra, its inverse map
\iota_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
is continuous if and only if
\mathchar 28675\relax^{\times}
is a
G_{\mathchar 28942\relax}
-set, i.e., if and only if it is a countable intersection of open subsets of
\mathchar 28675\relax

[
47
]
. We will show that
\mathchar 28675\relax^{\times}
is actually open in the specific Fréchet
{\mathbb{R}}
-algebra
\mathchar 28675\relax
considered. In view of Equation (16) in
[
14
]
, the topology of
\mathchar 28675\relax={\mathbb{R}}[[\theta]]
(
\mathchar 28675\relax\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{0|\underline{m}}
) is induced by the countable family of seminorms

\mathchar 28954\relax_{\mathchar 28940\relax}(\mathchar 28949\relax)=\frac{1}{\mathchar 28940\relax!}|\mathchar 28962\relax(\partial^{\mathchar 28940\relax}_{\theta}\mathchar 28949\relax)|=|\mathchar 28949\relax_{\mathchar 28940\relax}|\qquad\bigg(\mathchar 28940\relax\in\mathbb{N}^{\times|\underline{m}|},\ \mathchar 28949\relax=\sum_{\mathchar 28939\relax}\mathchar 28949\relax_{\mathchar 28939\relax}\theta^{\mathchar 28939\relax}\in\mathchar 28675\relax\bigg),

where
\mathchar 28962\relax
is the projection
\mathchar 28962\relax\colon\mathchar 28675\relax\to{\mathbb{R}}
. This means that the topology is made of the unions of finite intersections of the open semiballs

B_{\mathchar 28940\relax}(\mathchar 28951\relax,\mathchar 28962\relax)=\{\mathchar 28949\relax\in\mathchar 28675\relax\colon\mathchar 28954\relax_{\mathchar 28940\relax}(\mathchar 28949\relax-\mathchar 28951\relax)<\mathchar 28962\relax\}=\{\mathchar 28949\relax\in\mathchar 28675\relax\colon|\mathchar 28949\relax_{\mathchar 28940\relax}-\mathchar 28951\relax_{\mathchar 28940\relax}|<\mathchar 28962\relax\}=\{\mathchar 28949\relax\in\mathchar 28675\relax\colon\mathchar 28949\relax_{\mathchar 28940\relax}\in b(\mathchar 28951\relax_{\mathchar 28940\relax},\mathchar 28962\relax)\}

\big(\mathchar 28940\relax\in\mathbb{N}^{\times|\underline{m}|}
,
\mathchar 28951\relax=\sum_{\mathchar 28939\relax}\mathchar 28951\relax_{\mathchar 28939\relax}\theta^{\mathchar 28939\relax}\in\mathchar 28675\relax
,
\mathchar 28962\relax>0
and
b(\mathchar 28951\relax_{\mathchar 28940\relax},\mathchar 28962\relax)
is the open ball in
{\mathbb{R}}
with center
\mathchar 28951\relax_{\mathchar 28940\relax}
and radius
\mathchar 28962\relax\big)
. Since

\mathchar 28675\relax^{\times}=\{\mathchar 28949\relax\in\mathchar 28675\relax\colon\mathchar 28949\relax_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}=\bigcup_{r\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}}b(r,\mathchar 28962\relax_{r})\qquad(\text{for some}\ \mathchar 28962\relax_{r}>0),

we get

\mathchar 28675\relax^{\times}=\bigcup_{r\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}}\{\mathchar 28949\relax\in\mathchar 28675\relax\colon\mathchar 28949\relax_{0}\in b(r,\mathchar 28962\relax_{r})\}=\bigcup_{r\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}}B_{0}(r,\mathchar 28962\relax_{r}),

which implies that
\mathchar 28675\relax^{\times}
is open and that
\iota_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
is continuous, as announced.

Before we are able to deduce from this that
\operatorname{inv}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
is continuous, we need an inversion formula for
X\in\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)
. Notice first that, in view of
[
23
, Proposition 4.7]
, an invertible
2\times 2
block matrix

X=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}

with square diagonal blocks
A
and
D
and entries (of all blocks) in a ring, has a block UDL decomposition if and only if
D
is invertible. In this case, the UDL decomposition is

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\[2.0pt]
C&D\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{I}&BD^{-1}\\[2.0pt]
0&\mathbb{I}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}A-BD^{-1}C&0\\[2.0pt]
0&D\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{I}&0\\[2.0pt]
D^{-1}C&\mathbb{I}\end{pmatrix}\!.

As upper and lower unitriangular matrices are obviously invertible, it follows that the diagonal matrix is invertible, hence that
A-BD^{-1}C
is invertible. Similarly, the invertible matrix
X
has a block LDU decomposition if and only if
A
is invertible and in this case
D-CA^{-1}B
is invertible. Moreover, in view of Proposition
3.1
, a matrix
X\in\operatorname{gl}_{0}\big(p|\underline{q},\mathchar 28675\relax\big)
is invertible if and only if all its diagonal blocks
X_{ii}
are invertible. Let now

X=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}

be a
2\times 2
block decomposition of
X\in\operatorname{gl}_{0}\big(p|\underline{q},\mathchar 28675\relax\big)
that respects the
(N+1)\times(N+1)
block decomposition

X=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}X_{00}&\dotsc&X_{0N}\\
\hline\cr\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\hline\cr X_{N0}&\dotsc&X_{NN}\\
\end{array}\right)\!.

Since
A
(resp.,
D
) is invertible if and only if

\tilde{A}=\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&\mathbb{I}\end{pmatrix}\quad\quad\left(\text{resp.,}\;\tilde{D}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{I}&0\\
0&D\end{pmatrix}\right)

is invertible, hence, if and only if the
X_{kk}
on the diagonal of
A
(resp.,
D
) are invertible, we get that
X
is invertible if and only if
A
and
D
are invertible. If we combine everything we have said so far in this paragraph, we find that if
X\in\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)
, then
A,D,A-BD^{-1}C,D-CA^{-1}B
are all invertible. Therefore, we can use the formula

X^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\big(A-BD^{-1}C\big)^{-1}&-A^{-1}B\big(D-CA^{-1}B\big)^{-1}\\
-D^{-1}C\big(A-BD^{-1}C\big)^{-1}&\big(D-CA^{-1}B\big)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\!,

(3.13)

for any
X\in\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)
.

In order to simplify proper understanding, we consider for instance the case
n=2
,

p|\underline{q}=p|q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}=1|2,1,1

and

X=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}a&b\;c&d&e\\
\hline\cr f&g\;h&i&j\\
k&l\;m&n&p\\
\hline\cr q&r\;s&t&u\\
\hline\cr v&w\;x&y&z\\
\end{array}\right)\in\operatorname{GL}(1|2,1,1)(\mathchar 28675\relax),\qquad\text{where}\quad A=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}a&b\;c\\
\hline\cr f&g\;h\\
k&l\;m\\
\end{array}\right)\!,

and so on. We focus for instance on the first of the four block matrices in
X^{-1}
, i.e., on
\big(A-BD^{-1}C\big)^{-1}
. The matrix
D
is a
2\times 2
invertible matrix with square diagonal blocks and entries in
\mathchar 28675\relax
. Since the four diagonal block matrices in
X
are invertible, it follows from what we have said above that the inverse
D^{-1}
is given by equation (
3.13
) with
A=t\in\mathchar 28675\relax
,
B=u\in\mathchar 28675\relax
,
C=y\in\mathchar 28675\relax
and
D=z\in\mathchar 28675\relax
. Hence all entries of
D^{-1}
are composites of the addition, the subtraction, the multiplication and the inverse in
\mathchar 28675\relax
, and so are all entries in the invertible
2\times 2
block matrix

A-BD^{-1}C=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}\mathchar 28939\relax&\mathchar 28940\relax\;\mathchar 28941\relax\\
\hline\cr\mathchar 28942\relax&\mathchar 28962\relax\;\zeta\\
\mathchar 28945\relax&\mathchar 28946\relax\;\mathchar 28952\relax\\
\end{array}\right)\;

(3.14)

with square diagonal blocks (which are invertible) and with entries in
\mathchar 28675\relax
(the square diagonal blocks have entries in
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
). Hence, the inverse
(A-BD^{-1}C)^{-1}
can again be computed by (
3.13
). We focus on its entry

\displaystyle\mathchar 28948\relax:=\left(\mathchar 28939\relax-(\mathchar 28940\relax\,\mathchar 28941\relax)\begin{pmatrix}\mathchar 28962\relax&\zeta\\
\mathchar 28946\relax&\mathchar 28952\relax\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\mathchar 28942\relax\\
\mathchar 28945\relax\end{pmatrix}\right)^{-1}\in\mathchar 28675\relax.

(3.15)

Notice that here we cannot conclude that
\mathchar 28962\relax
and
\mathchar 28952\relax
are invertible and apply (
3.13
) to compute the internal inverse. However, this inverse is the inverse of a square matrix with entries in the commutative ring
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
, for which the standard inversion formula holds (recall that a square matrix with entries in a commutative ring is invertible if and only if its determinant is invertible):

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathchar 28962\relax&\zeta\\
\mathchar 28946\relax&\mathchar 28952\relax\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=(\mathchar 28962\relax\mathchar 28952\relax-\zeta\mathchar 28946\relax)^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\mathchar 28952\relax&-\zeta\\
-\mathchar 28946\relax&\mathchar 28962\relax\end{pmatrix}\!.

(3.16)

Since all the entries of (
3.14
) are composites of the addition, subtraction, multiplication and inverse in
\mathchar 28675\relax
, it follows from (
3.15
) and (
3.16
) that the same is true for the entry
\mathchar 28948\relax
of
X^{-1}
. More precisely the entry
\mathchar 28948\relax
corresponds to a map
\tilde{\mathchar 28948\relax}
that is a composite of the inclusion of
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)
into its topological supspace
\mathchar 28675\relax^{\times(t+|\underline{u}|)}
(continuous), the projection of
\mathchar 28675\relax^{\times(t+|\underline{u}|)}
onto
\mathchar 28675\relax^{\times v}
(
v\leq t+|\underline{u}|
) (continuous) and of products of the identity map
\operatorname{id}
of
\mathchar 28675\relax
(continuous), the diagonal map
\mathchar 28673\relax
of
\mathchar 28675\relax
(continuous), the switching map
\mathchar 28955\relax
of
\mathchar 28675\relax\times\mathchar 28675\relax
(continuous), the addition
a
of
\mathchar 28675\relax
(continuous), the scalar multiplication
e
of
\mathchar 28675\relax
(continuous), its subtraction
s
(continuous), multiplication
m
(continuous) and its inverse
\iota
(continuous). Indeed, it is for instance easily seen that the map

\mathchar 28675\relax^{\times 4}\ni(t,u,y,z)\mapsto-z^{-1}y\big(t-uz^{-1}y\big)^{-1}\in\mathchar 28675\relax

is a (continuous) composite of products of these continuous maps. We thus understand that the entry
\mathchar 28948\relax
of
X^{-1}
corresponds to a continuous map
\tilde{\mathchar 28948\relax}\colon\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)\to\mathchar 28675\relax
. The same holds of course also for all the other entries of
X^{-1}
. Finally, the inverse map

\operatorname{inv}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\colon\ \operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)\ni X\mapsto X^{-1}\in\mathchar 28675\relax^{\times(t+|\underline{u}|)}

is continuous and it remains continuous when view as valued in the subspace
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)
.
∎

Example 3.5
.

In view of equations (
3.10
), (
3.9
) and (
3.8
), the general linear
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{2}
-group of order
1|1,1,1
is

\operatorname{GL}(1|1,1,1)\simeq\left(({\mathbb{R}}^{\times})^{4},\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4|4,4,4}}|_{({\mathbb{R}}^{\times})^{4}}\right),

where
{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}={\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
.

3.5
Smooth linear actions

In this section we define linear actions of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-Lie groups
G
on finite dimensional
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-vector spaces
\mathbf{V}\simeq V
(we identify the isomorphic categories
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt FinVec}
and
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt LinMan}
). The definition can be given in the category of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifolds, but it is slightly more straightforward if we use the functor of points. Notice that the functors of points of
G\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Lg}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man}
and
V\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt LinMan}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man}
are functors

\mathcal{S}(G)=G(-)\in{\tt Fun}_{0}\big({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt AFLg}\big)\subset{\tt Fun}_{0}\big({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt AFM}\big)

and

\mathcal{S}(V)=V(-)\in{\tt Fun}_{0}\big({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt FAMod}\big)\subset{\tt Fun}_{0}\big({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt AFM}\big).

Definition 3.6
.

Let
G\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Lg}
and
V\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt LinMan}
. A
smooth linear action
of
G
on
V
is a natural transformation

\sigma_{-}\colon\ (G\times V)(-)=G(-)\times V(-)\rightarrow V(-)

in
{\tt Fun}_{0}\big({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt AFM}\big)
(natural transformation with
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-smooth
\mathchar 28675\relax
-components) that satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

Identity: for all
v_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\in V(\mathchar 28675\relax)
, we have

\sigma_{\Lambda}(1_{\Lambda},v_{\Lambda})=v_{\Lambda},

where
1_{\Lambda}
is the unit of
G(\mathchar 28675\relax)
.

(ii)

Compatibility: for all
g_{\Lambda},g^{\prime}_{\Lambda}\in G(\Lambda)
and all
v_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\in V(\mathchar 28675\relax)
, we have

\sigma_{\Lambda}\big(g_{\Lambda},\sigma_{\Lambda}(g^{\prime}_{\Lambda},v_{\Lambda})\big)=\sigma_{\Lambda}\big(\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}(g_{\Lambda},g^{\prime}_{\Lambda}),v_{\Lambda}\big),

where
\mathchar 28950\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
is the multiplication of
G(\mathchar 28675\relax)
.

(iii)

\Lambda_{0}
-linearity: for all
g_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\in G(\mathchar 28675\relax)
, all
v_{\Lambda},v^{\prime}_{\Lambda}\in V(\Lambda)
and all
\mathrm{a}\in\Lambda_{0}
, we have

(a)

\sigma_{\Lambda}(g_{\Lambda},v_{\Lambda}+v^{\prime}_{\Lambda})=\sigma_{\Lambda}(g_{\Lambda},v_{\Lambda})+\sigma_{\Lambda}(g_{\Lambda},v^{\prime}_{\Lambda})
,

(b)

\sigma_{\Lambda}(g_{\Lambda},\mathrm{a}\cdot v_{\Lambda})=\mathrm{a}\cdot\sigma_{\Lambda}(g_{\Lambda},v_{\Lambda})
,

where
\cdot
is the action of
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
on
V(\mathchar 28675\relax)
.

Since

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\colon\ {\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man}\to{\tt Fun}_{0}\big({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt AFM}\big)

(3.17)

is fully faithful (for more details, see
[
13
,
14
,
15
]
), there is a
1:1
correspondence between natural transformations
\mathchar 28955\relax_{-}
as above and
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphisms

\mathchar 28955\relax\colon\ G\times V\to V.

This correspondence implies in particular that condition (
ii
) is equivalent to the equality

\displaystyle\mathchar 28955\relax\circ(\operatorname{id}_{G}\times\mathchar 28955\relax)=\mathchar 28955\relax\circ(\mathchar 28950\relax\times\operatorname{id}_{V})

(3.18)

of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphisms from
G\times G\times V\to V
(
\mathchar 28950\relax\colon G\times G\to G
is the multiplication of
G
). The same holds for condition (
i
) and the equality

\displaystyle\mathchar 28955\relax\circ(e\times\operatorname{id}_{V})=\operatorname{id}_{V}

(3.19)

of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphisms from
V\simeq 1\times V\to V
(
e\colon 1\to G
is the two-sided unit of
\mathchar 28950\relax
).

3.5.1
Canonical action of the general linear group

We will now define the canonical action of the general linear
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-group
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)=\mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Lg}
on the Cartesian
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifold
{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt LinMan}
. To do this, we use both, the fully faithful functor (
3.17
) and the fully faithful functor

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}\colon\ {\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man}\ni M\mapsto\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man}}(-,M)\in{\tt Fun}\big({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt Set}\big).

(3.20)

We start defining a natural transformation
\mathchar 28955\relax_{-}
of
{\tt Fun}\big({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt Set}\big)
from
\mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}(-)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}(-)
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}(-)
. We will denote the coordinates of
\mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}
(resp.,
{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}
) here by
\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{b}}
(resp.,
\mathcal{x}^{\mathfrak{c}}
), where
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\in\{1,\dots,p+|\underline{q}|\}
(resp., where
\mathfrak{c}\in\{1,\dots,p+|\underline{q}|\}
). For this, we must associate to any
S\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man}
, a set-theoretical map
\mathchar 28955\relax_{S}
that assigns to any

(X,\mathchar 28958\relax)\in\mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}(S)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}(S)=\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man}}\big(S,\mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}\big)\times\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man}}\big(S,{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}\big),

i.e., to any (appropriate) coordinate pullbacks

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{S,\mathfrak{b}},\mathcal{x}_{S}^{\mathfrak{c}}\big):=(X^{*}(\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{b}}),\mathchar 28958\relax^{*}(\mathcal{x}^{\mathfrak{c}}))\in\mathcal{O}(S)^{\times(p+|\underline{q}|)^{2}}\times\mathcal{O}(S)^{\times(p+|\underline{q}|)},

(3.21)

a unique element
\mathchar 28955\relax_{S}(X,\mathchar 28958\relax)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}(S)
, i.e., unique (appropriate) coordinate pullbacks

\mathchar 28955\relax_{S}\big(\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{S,\mathfrak{b}},\mathcal{x}_{S}^{\mathfrak{c}}\big)\in\mathcal{O}(S)^{\times(p+|\underline{q}|)}.

Since
\big(\mathcal{x}_{S}^{\mathfrak{c}}\big)_{\mathfrak{c}}
is viewed as a tuple (horizontal row), the natural definition of this image (horizontal row) is

\displaystyle\mathchar 28955\relax_{S}\big(\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{S,\mathfrak{b}},\mathcal{x}_{S}^{\mathfrak{c}}\big)=\big(\mathcal{x}_{S}^{\mathfrak{b}}\;\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{S,\mathfrak{b}}\big)_{\mathfrak{a}},

(3.22)

where the sum and products are taken in the global
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-function algebra
\mathcal{O}(S)
of
S
. It is clear that the elements of this target-tuple have the required degrees, as the same holds for the elements of the source-tuple. The transformation
\mathchar 28955\relax_{-}
we just defined is clearly natural. Indeed, for any
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphism
\psi\colon S^{\prime}\to S
, the induced set-theoretical mapping between the Hom-sets with source
S
and the corresponding ones with source
S^{\prime}
is
-\circ\psi
, so that the induced set-theoretical mapping between the tuples of global
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-functions of
S
and
S^{\prime}
is
\psi^{*}
. The naturalness of
\mathchar 28955\relax_{-}
follows now from the fact that
\psi^{*}
is a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Alg}
-morphism.

Since (
3.20
) is fully faithful, the natural transformation
\mathchar 28955\relax_{-}
is implemented by a unique
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphism

\displaystyle\mathchar 28955\relax\colon\ \operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}},

(3.23)

which in turn implements, via (
3.17
), a unique natural transformation in
{\tt Fun}_{0}\big({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt AFM}\big)
between the same functors, but restricted to
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}}
. Since this transformation is the restriction of
\mathchar 28955\relax_{-}
to
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Pts}^{\operatorname{op}}
, we use this symbol for both transformations (provided that any confusion can be excluded). It is easily seen that

\mathchar 28955\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\big(\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathchar 28675\relax,\mathfrak{b}},\mathcal{x}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}^{\mathfrak{c}}\big)=\big(\mathcal{x}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}^{\mathfrak{b}}\;\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathchar 28675\relax,\mathfrak{b}}\big)_{\mathfrak{a}},

with sum and products in
\mathchar 28675\relax
, has the properties (
i
), (
ii
) and (
iii
) of Definition
3.6
, so that we defined a smooth linear action of
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}
.

The interesting aspect here is that we are able to compute the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphism (
3.23
). Indeed, in view of the proof of the full faithfulness of the standard Yoneda embedding
c\mapsto\operatorname{Hom}_{\tt C}(-,c)
of an arbitrary locally small category
\tt C
into the functor category
{\tt Fun}\big({\tt C}^{\operatorname{op}},{\tt Set}\big)
, the morphism
\mathchar 28955\relax\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\tt C}(c,c^{\prime})
that implements a natural transformation

\mathchar 28955\relax_{-}\colon\ \operatorname{Hom}_{\tt C}(-,c)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\tt C}(-,c^{\prime})

is

\mathchar 28955\relax=\mathchar 28955\relax_{c}(\operatorname{id}_{c})\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\tt C}(c,c^{\prime}).

In our case of interest
{\tt C}={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man}
, the previous Yoneda embedding is the functor (
3.20
) and the morphism

\mathchar 28955\relax\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}\tt Man}\big(\!\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}},{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}\big)

is

\mathchar 28955\relax=\mathchar 28955\relax_{c}(\operatorname{id}_{c}),\qquad\text{with}\quad c=\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}.

Since the pullback of the identity
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphism
\operatorname{id}_{c}
is identity and the coordinate pullbacks (
3.21
) are

\big(\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{b}},\mathcal{x}^{\mathfrak{c}}\big)\in\mathcal{O}(c)^{\times(p+|\underline{q}|)(p+|\underline{q}|+1)}.

Equation (
3.22
) yields

\mathchar 28955\relax=\mathchar 28955\relax_{c}(\operatorname{id}_{c})\simeq\mathchar 28955\relax_{c}\big(\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{b}},\mathcal{x}^{\mathfrak{c}}\big)=\big(\mathcal{x}^{\mathfrak{b}}\,\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{b}}\big)_{\mathfrak{a}},

with sum and products in
\mathcal{O}(c)
. In other words:

Proposition 3.7
.

The canonical action
\mathchar 28955\relax
of the general linear
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-group
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)
on the linear
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifold or
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-graded vector space
{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}
, is the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphism that is defined by the coordinate pullbacks

\displaystyle\mathchar 28955\relax^{*}(\mathcal{x}^{\mathfrak{a}})=\mathcal{x}^{\mathfrak{b}}\,\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{b}},

(3.24)

where we denoted the coordinates of
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)

(
resp.,
{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}})
by
\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{b}}

(
resp.,
\mathcal{x}^{\mathfrak{c}})
.

Example 3.8
.

We know that the general linear
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{2}
-group
\GL(1|1,1,1)
can be identified with the open
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{2}
-submanifold
\mathcal{U}^{4|4,4,4}
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{4|4,4,4}
. We denote the global coordinates of this Cartesian
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{2}
-manifold by
\big(x^{\alpha},\mathchar 28952\relax^{\beta},\theta^{\gamma},z^{\delta}\big)
. The indices run over
\{1,2,3,4\}
and the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{2}
-degrees of these coordinates are
(0,0)
,
(0,1)
,
(1,0)
and
(1,1)
, respectively. We already mentioned that if we view
\mathcal{U}^{4|4,4,4}
as
\operatorname{GL}(1|1,1,1)
, we must rearrange the coordinates:

\mathcal{X}=(\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{b}})_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}=\left(\begin{matrix}x^{1}&\mathchar 28952\relax^{1}&\theta^{1}&z^{1}\\
\mathchar 28952\relax^{2}&x^{2}&z^{2}&\theta^{2}\\
\theta^{3}&z^{3}&x^{3}&\mathchar 28952\relax^{3}\\
z^{4}&\theta^{4}&\mathchar 28952\relax^{4}&x^{4}\\
\end{matrix}\right)\!.

In view of (
3.24
), the action
\mathchar 28955\relax
of
\operatorname{GL}(1|1,1,1)
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1,1,1}
with global coordinates

\mathcal{x}=\big(\mathcal{x}^{\mathfrak{a}}\big)_{\mathfrak{a}}=\big(x^{0},\mathchar 28952\relax^{0},\theta^{0},z^{0}\big),

is given as

\displaystyle\sigma^{*}\big(x^{0}\big)=x^{0}x^{1}+\mathchar 28952\relax^{0}\mathchar 28952\relax^{1}+\theta^{0}\theta^{1}+z^{0}z^{1},

\displaystyle\sigma^{*}\big(\mathchar 28952\relax^{0}\big)=x^{0}\mathchar 28952\relax^{2}+\mathchar 28952\relax^{0}x^{2}+\theta^{0}z^{2}+z^{0}\theta^{2},

\displaystyle\sigma^{*}\big(\theta^{0}\big)=x^{0}\theta^{3}+\mathchar 28952\relax^{0}z^{3}+\theta^{0}x^{3}+z^{0}\mathchar 28952\relax^{3},

\displaystyle\sigma^{*}\big(z^{0}\big)=x^{0}z^{4}+\mathchar 28952\relax^{0}\theta^{4}+\theta^{0}\mathchar 28952\relax^{4}+z^{0}x^{4}.

3.5.2
Connection between the canonical action and the internal Hom

Since

\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)=\mathcal{U}^{t|\underline{u}}

(see equation (
3.10
)) is an open
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-submanifold (see equation (
3.9
)) of

\operatorname{gl}_{0}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)={\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt LinMan}}\big({\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}},{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}\big)

(see Proposition
2.29
and equation (
2.63
)), we can expect a connection between the canonical action of
\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}
and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt LinMan}}\big({\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}},{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}\big)
. It turns out that this link becomes apparent as soon as we understand the connection between the internal Hom of linear
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifolds and the internal Hom of arbitrary
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifolds. Indeed, for any
\mathchar 28675\relax\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{0|\underline{m}}
, we have

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man}}\big({\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}},{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax):=\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man}}\big({\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}},{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}\big)

(see
[
13
]
). If we denote the coordinates of
{\mathbb{R}}^{0|\underline{m}}
by
\mathchar 28946\relax=(\mathchar 28946\relax^{\mathfrak{d}})
and those of
{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}
by
\mathcal{x}=(\mathcal{x}^{\mathfrak{a}})=\big(x^{a},\mathchar 28952\relax^{A}\big)
, the
RHS
Hom-set can be identified with the set of (degree respecting) coordinate pullbacks:

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man}}\big({\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}},{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)=\bigg\{\mathcal{x}^{\mathfrak{a}}=\mathcal{x}^{\mathfrak{a}}(x,\mathchar 28952\relax,\mathchar 28946\relax)=\sum_{\mathchar 28939\relax\mathchar 28940\relax}f^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathchar 28939\relax\mathchar 28940\relax}(x)\mathchar 28952\relax^{\mathchar 28939\relax}\mathchar 28946\relax^{\mathchar 28940\relax}\bigg\}.

On the other hand, when denoting the coordinates of
{\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}
as above by
\mathcal{X}=(\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{b}})
, we get similarly

\displaystyle\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt LinMan}}\big({\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}},{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)=\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Man}}\big(\mathchar 28675\relax,{\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}\big)

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{b}}=\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\mathchar 28946\relax)=\sum_{\mathchar 28942\relax}r^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{b},\mathchar 28942\relax}\,\mathchar 28946\relax^{\mathchar 28942\relax}\bigg\}=\operatorname{gl}_{0}\big(p|\underline{q},\mathchar 28675\relax\big).

(3.25)

An obvious identification leads now to

\displaystyle\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt LinMan}}\big({\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}},{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\bigg\{\mathcal{x}^{\mathfrak{a}}=\mathcal{x}^{\mathfrak{a}}(x,\mathchar 28952\relax,\mathchar 28946\relax)=\sum_{\mathfrak{b}}\mathcal{x}^{\mathfrak{b}}\,\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\mathchar 28946\relax)=\sum_{b}x^{b}\,\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{b}(\mathchar 28946\relax)+\sum_{B}\mathchar 28952\relax^{B}\,\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{B}(\mathchar 28946\relax)\bigg\}.

(3.26)

When comparing (
3.26
) and (
3.25
), we see that the internal Hom of linear
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifolds consists of the pullbacks of the internal Hom of arbitrary
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifolds which are defined by the canonical action of
\operatorname{gl}_{0}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{p|\underline{q}}
, in the sense of (
3.24
).

3.5.3
Equivalent definitions of a smooth linear action

Section
3.5
already implicitly contained the idea that a smooth linear action of a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-Lie group
G
on a linear
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifold
V
in the sense of Definition
3.6
, is equivalent to a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphism
\mathchar 28955\relax\colon G\times V\to V
that satisfies the conditions (
3.18
) and (
3.19
) and additionally has a certain linearity property with respect to
V
. A natural idea is that
\mathchar 28955\relax^{*}
should send linear
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-functions of
V
to
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-functions of
G\times V
that are linear along the fibers. The meaning of this concept becomes clear when we think of the classical differential geometric case in which the functions of a trivial vector bundle
E=M\times{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
are

C^{\infty}(E)=\mathchar 28672\relax(\vee E^{*})=C^{\infty}(M)\otimes\,\vee\,(\mathbf{R}^{r})^{*}

(
\vee
is the symmetric tensor product), i.e., are the functions that are smooth in the base and polynomial along the fiber. Hence, linear functions of
E
are the functions that are smooth in the base and linear along the fiber, i.e.,

C^{\infty}_{\operatorname{lin}}(E)=C^{\infty}(M)\otimes(\mathbf{R}^{r})^{*}=C^{\infty}(M)\otimes C^{\infty}_{\operatorname{lin}}({\mathbb{R}}^{r}).

We can choose the same definition in the case of the trivial
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-vector bundle
E=G\times V
:

\mathcal{O}^{\operatorname{lin}}_{E}(|G|\times|V|):=\mathcal{O}_{G}(|G|)\otimes\mathcal{O}^{\operatorname{lin}}_{V}(|V|).

This definition is of course in particular valid for
G=\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}{\tt Lg}
. However, let us mention that the linear functions (“linear along the fibers”) of the trivial
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-vector bundle
E=

\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)\times V
that are defined on
|\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)|\times|V|
do not coincide with the linear functions (“globally linear”) of the linear
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifold
M={\mathbb{R}}^{t|\underline{u}}\times V
(see (
2.57
)) that are defined on the open subset
|\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)|\times|V|
of its base
{\mathbb{R}}^{t}\times|V|
:

\mathcal{O}^{\operatorname{lin}}_{E}\big(\big|\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)\big|\times|V|\big)\neq\mathcal{O}_{M}^{\operatorname{lin}}\big(\big|\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)\big|\times|V|\big).

Given what we have just said, we expect the following proposition to hold:

Proposition 3.9
.

A smooth linear action
\mathchar 28955\relax_{-}
of the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-Lie group
G=\operatorname{GL}\big(p|\underline{q}\big)
on a linear
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifold
V
in the sense of Definition
3.6
, is equivalent to a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphism
\mathchar 28955\relax\colon G\times V\to V
that satisfies the conditions (
3.18
) and (
3.19
) and has the linearity property

\displaystyle\mathchar 28955\relax^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{V}^{\operatorname{lin}}(|V|))\subset\mathcal{O}_{G}(|G|)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{V}^{\operatorname{lin}}(|V|).

(3.27)

Notice first that the pullback
\mathchar 28955\relax^{*}
is a morphism of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-algebras

\mathchar 28955\relax^{*}\colon\ \mathcal{O}_{V}(|V|)\to\mathcal{O}_{G\times V}(|G|\times|V|).

Since
G
and
V
have global coordinates, it follows from
[
15
]
that the target of
\mathchar 28955\relax^{*}
is given by

\mathcal{O}_{G\times V}(|G|\times|V|)=\mathcal{O}_{G}(|G|)\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{O}_{V}(|V|),

which shows that it contains

\mathcal{O}_{G}(|G|)\otimes\,\mathcal{O}_{V}^{\operatorname{lin}}(|V|)

and that the requirement (
3.27
) actually makes sense.

Another fact is also worth noting. We know from standard supergeometry that the classical Berezinian defines a super-Lie group morphism

\operatorname{Ber}\colon\ \operatorname{GL}(p|q)\to\operatorname{GL}(1|0),

so that we get a linear action of
\operatorname{GL}(p|q)
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|0}
. The point here is that linear actions of
\operatorname{GL}(p|q)
are not limited to actions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{p|q}
.

Proof.

In the light of the observations that follow Definition
3.6
, it suffices to prove that the
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-linearity requirement (
iii
) in Definition
3.6
is equivalent to the linearity condition (
3.27
) in Proposition
3.9
. Hence, let
\mathchar 28955\relax_{-}
be a smooth action of
G
on
V
and let
\mathchar 28955\relax
be the corresponding
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphism. If
\textnormal{h}\colon V\to{\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}
is a linear coordinate map of
V
, the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphism

\mathfrak{S}:=\textnormal{h}\circ\mathchar 28955\relax\circ\big(\operatorname{id}_{G}\times\textnormal{h}^{-1}\big)\colon\ G\times{\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}

satisfies (
3.27
) if and only if the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphism
\mathchar 28955\relax
does. Indeed, if
\mathchar 28955\relax
has the property (
3.27
), then

\mathfrak{S}^{*}=\big(\operatorname{id}_{G}\times\textnormal{h}^{-1}\big)^{*}\circ\mathchar 28955\relax^{*}\circ\textnormal{h}^{*}=\big(\operatorname{id}_{G}^{*}\widehat{\otimes}\,\big(\textnormal{h}^{-1}\big)^{*}\big)\circ\mathchar 28955\relax^{*}\circ\textnormal{h}^{*}

has obviously the same property. We similarly find that the converse implication holds. On the other hand, if we denote the coordinates of
{\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}
by
\mathcal{y}=(\mathcal{y}^{\mathfrak{c}})=\big(y^{c},\eta^{C}\big)
, the
\mathchar 28675\relax
-components of the natural transformations
\mathchar 28955\relax_{-}
and
\mathfrak{S}_{-}
satisfy

\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}=\textnormal{h}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\circ\mathchar 28955\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\circ\big(\operatorname{id}_{G(\mathchar 28675\relax)}\times\textnormal{h}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}^{-1}\big)

and

\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\bigg(g_{\mathchar 28675\relax},\sum_{k}\mathchar 28949\relax^{k}\mathcal{y}_{\mathchar 28675\relax,k}\bigg)=\textnormal{h}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\bigg(\mathchar 28955\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\bigg(g_{\mathchar 28675\relax},\textnormal{h}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}^{-1}\bigg(\sum_{k}\mathchar 28949\relax^{k}\mathcal{y}_{\mathchar 28675\relax,k}\bigg)\bigg)\bigg),

for any
g_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\in G(\mathchar 28675\relax)
, any
\mathcal{y}_{\mathchar 28675\relax,k}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)
and any
\mathchar 28949\relax^{k}\in\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
(where
k
runs through a finite set). Since

\textnormal{h}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\colon\ V(\mathchar 28675\relax)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)

is an isomorphism of Fréchet
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-modules, the
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-smooth map
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
is
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-linear in
\mathcal{y}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
if and only if the
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-smooth map
\mathchar 28955\relax_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
is
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-linear in
v_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\in V(\mathchar 28675\relax)
. It is therefore sufficient to prove the equivalence “(
iii
) if and only if (
3.27
)” for
V={\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}
.

We refrain from writing down the proof of the implication “if (
iii
) then (
3.27
)”. It is technical and partially reminiscent of a part of the proof of Theorem
2.20
(for the super-case, see
[
17
]
and the references it contains).

We now prove the converse implication from scratch. Assume that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}^{*}\big(\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}}^{\operatorname{lin}}({\mathbb{R}}^{r})\big)\subset\mathcal{O}_{G}(|G|)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}}^{\operatorname{lin}}({\mathbb{R}}^{r}).

(3.28)

In view of the universal property of the product of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifolds, we have

G(\mathchar 28675\relax)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)\ni(g_{\mathchar 28675\relax},\mathcal{y}_{\mathchar 28675\relax})\simeq u_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\in\big(G\times{\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax).

If we identify the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-morphisms
g_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
,
\mathcal{y}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
,
u_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
with the corresponding continuous
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-algebra morphisms

\displaystyle g_{\mathchar 28675\relax}^{*}

\displaystyle\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}\tt Alg}\big(\mathcal{O}_{G}(|G|),\mathchar 28675\relax\big),\mathcal{y}^{*}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}\tt Alg}\big(\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}}({\mathbb{R}}^{r}),\mathchar 28675\relax\big),u_{\mathchar 28675\relax}^{*}

\displaystyle\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}\tt Alg}\big(\mathcal{O}_{G}(|G|)\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}}({\mathbb{R}}^{r}),\mathchar 28675\relax\big),

we get

u_{\mathchar 28675\relax}^{*}=\widehat{m}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\circ\big(g_{\mathchar 28675\relax}^{*}\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{y}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}^{*}\big),

where
\widehat{m}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\colon\mathchar 28675\relax\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathchar 28675\relax\to\mathchar 28675\relax
is continuous
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-algebra morphism that extends the multiplication
m_{\mathchar 28675\relax}=\cdot_{\mathchar 28675\relax}
of
\mathchar 28675\relax
(see
[
15
]
). We denote the coordinates of
G\times{\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}
as
\mathcal{z}=(\mathcal{z}^{\mathfrak{d}})=(\mathcal{X}^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{b}},\mathcal{y}^{\mathfrak{c}})
. Since

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\colon\ \big(G\times{\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}\big)(\mathchar 28675\relax)\ni u_{\mathchar 28675\relax}

\displaystyle\simeq(g_{\mathchar 28675\relax},\mathcal{y}_{\mathchar 28675\relax})\simeq u^{*}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\simeq u^{*}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}(\mathcal{z}^{\mathfrak{d}})\mapsto\mathfrak{S}\circ u_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\simeq u_{\mathchar 28675\relax}^{*}\circ\mathfrak{S}^{*}

\displaystyle\simeq u_{\mathchar 28675\relax}^{*}(\mathfrak{S}^{*}(\mathcal{y}^{\mathfrak{c}}))\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}(\mathchar 28675\relax),

we find that

\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\bigg(g_{\mathchar 28675\relax},\sum_{k}\mathchar 28949\relax^{k}\mathcal{y}_{\mathchar 28675\relax,k}\bigg)\simeq\widehat{m}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\bigg(\bigg(g_{\mathchar 28675\relax}^{*}\widehat{\otimes}\bigg(\sum_{k}\mathchar 28949\relax^{k}\mathcal{y}_{\mathchar 28675\relax,k}\bigg)^{*}\bigg)\big(\mathfrak{S}^{*}(\mathcal{y}^{\mathfrak{c}})\big)\bigg).

In view of the definition of the
\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
-module structure on
{\mathbb{R}}^{r|\underline{s}}(\mathchar 28675\relax)
, we have

\bigg(\sum_{k}\mathchar 28949\relax^{k}\mathcal{y}_{\mathchar 28675\relax,k}\bigg)^{*}=\sum_{k}\mathchar 28949\relax^{k}\mathcal{y}_{\mathchar 28675\relax,k}^{*}

and in view of the assumption (
3.28
), we get for any
fixed

\mathfrak{c}
that

\mathfrak{S}^{*}(\mathcal{y}^{\mathfrak{c}})=\sum_{n=1}^{M}s_{G}^{n}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\bigg(\sum_{\mathfrak{c^{\prime}}}r^{n}_{\mathfrak{c^{\prime}}}\mathcal{y}^{\mathfrak{c^{\prime}}}\bigg),

where
M\in\mathbb{N}
, where
s_{G}^{n}\in\mathcal{O}_{G}(|G|)
and where
r^{n}_{\mathfrak{c^{\prime}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Since
\mathchar 28949\relax^{k}\in\mathchar 28675\relax_{0}
, what we just said yields

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\bigg(\!g_{\mathchar 28675\relax},\sum_{k}\mathchar 28949\relax^{k}\mathcal{y}_{\mathchar 28675\relax,k}\bigg)

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{n=1}^{M}g^{*}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}(s_{G}^{n})\cdot_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\sum_{k}\mathchar 28949\relax^{k}\cdot_{\mathchar 28675\relax}\!\bigg(\!\sum_{\mathfrak{c^{\prime}}}r^{n}_{\mathfrak{c^{\prime}}}\,\mathcal{y}_{\mathchar 28675\relax,k}^{*}(\mathcal{y}^{\mathfrak{c^{\prime}}})\bigg)

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{k}\mathchar 28949\relax^{k}\,\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchar 28675\relax}(g_{\mathchar 28675\relax},\mathcal{y}_{\mathchar 28675\relax,k}).

∎

4
Future directions

We view the current paper as the first steps towards understanding actions of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-Lie groups on
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifolds and we claim that it will be vital in carefully constructing the total spaces of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-vector bundles, for example. In both these settings, the functor of points, and in particular
\Lambda
-points, are expected to be of fundamental importance. In particular, the typical fibres of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-vector bundles cannot be
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-graded vector spaces, but rather they are linear
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifolds. Moreover, the transition functions will correspond to an action of the general linear
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-group and as such a careful understanding of linear actions is needed. This paper provides some of this technical background. We plan to explore the algebraic and geometric definitions of vector bundles in the category of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifolds in a future publication.

Appendix A
The category of modules over a variable algebra

We define the category
\tt AMod
(resp.,
\tt FAMod
) of modules (resp., Fréchet modules) over any (unital) algebra (resp., any (unital) Fréchet algebra)
A
. The algebra
A
can vary from object to object. The objects are the modules over some
A
(resp., the Fréchet vector spaces that come equipped with a (compatible) continuous
A
-action). We denote such modules by
M_{A}
. Morphisms consist of pairs
(\varphi,\Phi)
, where

\varphi\colon\ A\longrightarrow B

is an algebra morphism (resp., a continuous algebra morphism), and

\Phi\colon\ M_{A}\longrightarrow M_{B}

is a map (resp., a continuous map) that satisfies

\Phi(am+a^{\prime}m^{\prime})=\varphi(a)\Phi(m)+\varphi(a^{\prime})\Phi(m^{\prime}),

for all
a,a^{\prime}\in A
and
m,m^{\prime}\in M_{A}
. It is evident that we do indeed obtain a category in this way.

The preceding categories
\tt AMod
and
\tt FAMod
are similar to the category
\tt AFMan
that we used in
[
13
]
. They naturally appear when considering the zero degree rules functor or the functor of points. See for instance equations (
2.1
) and (
2.22
).

Appendix B
Basics of
\boldsymbol{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}}
-geometry

B.1
\boldsymbol{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}}
-manifolds and their morphisms

The locally ringed space approach to
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-manifolds was pioneered in
[
18
]
. We work over the field
{\mathbb{R}}
of real numbers and set
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}:={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times\dots\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
(
n
-times). A
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n}
-graded algebra
is an
{\mathbb{R}}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
with a decomposition into vector spaces
